14:I[8669,["/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/08cykig6vtx_p.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/07qi2okp-.yp9.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0frujd.eyhz4o.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0n4z.7yu76je-.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/09-o.77-p0p36.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/1304s0wcp2kce.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0j1uy9t~zxcf0.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/14_5o-nbg8k_r.js"],"DateSidebar"]
15:I[44692,["/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/08cykig6vtx_p.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/07qi2okp-.yp9.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0frujd.eyhz4o.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0n4z.7yu76je-.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/09-o.77-p0p36.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/1304s0wcp2kce.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0j1uy9t~zxcf0.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/14_5o-nbg8k_r.js"],"DailyView"]
16:T462,First, controlled experiments verify the universality of the small-vs-large gap: it persists across architectures including MLP and Transformer, optimizers including SGD and full-batch gradient descent, and tasks ranging from algorithmic to reasoning. The gap remains under full-batch updates, eliminating explanations based on gradient stochasticity. The core mechanism proposed is that smaller datasets inherently induce stronger sampling biases, which modulate the relative update magnitude across layers and form a layer-wise weight growth rhythm more conducive to feature learning, thus accelerating convergence. This convergence advantage is formally proven for MLP settings via theoretical derivation. Two types of intervention experiments further validate the mechanism: 1) Training on a small dataset with random labels still achieves similar speedup, ruling out the influence of task-relevant signals; 2) Adjusting initialization scales or layer-wise learning rates can significantly reduce or even eliminate the small-vs-large gap, confirming that relative layer update magnitude is the core mediating variable.17:T4f4,The framework defines three measurements. The first is scaling-law fit error, which evaluates how well a functional form predicts the optimal hyperparameter across scale. The second is a transfer robustness exponent κ, which measures whether errors made during small-to-large extrapolation become more or less harmful at larger scale. The third is asymptotic loss degradation R(∞), which checks whether a parameterization obtains cleaner transfer only by sacrificing final loss. The authors then express standard parameterization and μP in a common GPT-pretraining form and isolate four differences: the embedding learning rate changes from Θ(1/n) to Θ(1), the final-layer initialization variance changes from Θ(1/n) to Θ(1/n²), the LayerNorm learning rate changes from Θ(1/n) to Θ(1), and attention scaling changes from 1/√d to 1/d. A full 2^4 ablation over these switches shows that changing only the embedding learning rate in standard parameterization is enough to largely match μP’s transfer behavior. Training-dynamics analysis further shows that the embedding learning rate matters throughout training, with especially high sensitivity early on. Too small an embedding learning rate throttles representation learning and can trigger instability.18:T5c9,HRM-Text is built on a Hierarchical Recurrent Model with two timescales. The fast L module performs local iterative refinement, while the slow H module maintains a more stable semantic state. In the described forward pass, the model runs two high-level cycles; each cycle applies three L-module updates followed by one H-module update. Token logits are produced from the final H state. This reuses parameters across recurrent steps, increasing effective computational depth without increasing parameter count proportionally.

The difficult part is making deep recurrence trainable for language modeling. The paper introduces MagicNorm and warmup deep credit assignment. At the beginning of training, gradients are backpropagated only through the final two recurrent steps; later this window expands to the final five steps. This reduces the optimization burden early on and gradually exposes the model to longer credit-assignment paths. The modules also use parameterless RMSNorm, SwiGLU, RoPE, and sigmoid-gated self-attention.

The training objective departs from standard causal LM pretraining. HRM-Text is trained from scratch on instruction-response pairs, optimizing negative log-likelihood only on the response, i.e. P(answer | instruction). It uses a PrefixLM mask: instruction tokens can attend bidirectionally to one another, while response tokens remain causal. This is a better match to conditional generation than forcing the model to predict the prompt left-to-right.19:T427,RoPeSLR is a 3D RoPE-guided Sparse-LowRank attention framework. The paper argues that, under empirically supported assumptions, the post-softmax attention matrix in DiTs can be decomposed into a sparse high-frequency semantic spike component and a low-rank background continuum. Using the Fourier structure induced by 3D RoPE, the claimed scaling is O(L^{3/2}) for the spike set and O(d_h log L) for the low-rank background. The implementation separates responsibilities. A block-sparse attention branch, such as VMoBA, provides the main speedup and handles local or salient dependencies. A nonlinear low-rank MLP compensator restores smooth global context. Instead of using a standard positive-feature-map linear attention branch, the compensator is implemented as an amortized token-wise MLP conditioned on token features and explicit 3D absolute coordinates. A learnable 3D absolute positional embedding is injected to help recover distance decay that would otherwise be lost. A distribution-aware gated fusion module combines the sparse and low-rank branches.1a:T7b3,The core idea is to train a neural estimator that predicts pairwise conditional mutual information, I(X_i; X_j | C), from the hidden states of a pretrained masked diffusion model. Two aspects are important. First, the supervision comes from the model itself rather than external labels. Second, the estimator predicts the full pairwise MI matrix in one pass rather than computing each pair separately.

The motivation is straightforward. Standard MDM training learns marginals such as p(x_i | x_t), which are enough for denoising but not enough for deciding which positions can be safely sampled in parallel. To recover dependence information, the authors derive ground-truth MI targets from the model’s own conditional distributions and then train an auxiliary network to regress those MI values from hidden states. This avoids the usual difficulty of high-dimensional density estimation in classical MI estimation and avoids expensive pairwise enumeration at inference time.

From the paper’s description, the estimator takes position-wise hidden representations from the MDM backbone and outputs an L by L matrix of pairwise MI predictions. That is a practical design for inference because it turns dependency estimation into a single structured prediction problem rather than O(L^2) separate queries.

The estimated MI is then used in two ways. First, for interpretation: the authors visualize which positions the model considers strongly dependent in Sudoku and protein sequences. Second, for MI-guided parallel decoding: they sample together only subsets of variables that are approximately conditionally independent, instead of relying on confidence-based unmasking. This is the main distinction from Mask-Predict-style heuristics and entropy-based dynamic masking. Entropy measures single-position uncertainty; it does not explicitly represent pairwise conditional dependence, which is exactly what matters when deciding whether simultaneous updates are safe.1b:T62a,The experiments are conducted on two domains: Sudoku and protein sequences. Sudoku is a strongly constrained discrete reasoning problem with clear dependency structure. Protein sequences represent long discrete chains where parallel conditional sampling is practically meaningful. The experimental goal is not to maximize a generic generation benchmark, but to test two claims: whether the estimator can recover the model’s internal dependency structure, and whether that structure improves parallel decoding decisions.

From the available full-text content, the paper shows that the estimator can predict pairwise MI maps that align with task structure. In Sudoku, cells that should be tightly coupled exhibit stronger predicted dependence. In proteins, positions with stronger functional or structural coupling show higher conditional dependence. More importantly, MI-guided decoding appears to preserve consistency better than confidence- or entropy-based strategies during parallel sampling, reducing errors caused by unmasking strongly dependent variables together.

That said, the scope should be interpreted carefully. This is more of a mechanism and feasibility paper than a large-scale foundation-model result. The abstract mentions Sudoku and ESM-C protein sequences, but the provided full-text excerpt does not include enough concrete numbers on model size, speedup factors, or exact quality gains to support stronger claims. The safe reading is that the paper provides a workable dependency-estimation framework and validates it in two structured discrete domains.1c:T4cc,For a skim, start with the introduction and preliminaries to pin down the target quantity: this is not generic MI, but pairwise conditional MI under the current masked context. Then go to the method section and focus on two things: how the ground-truth MI targets are derived from the pretrained MDM’s conditional distributions, and how the estimator predicts the full MI matrix from hidden states. That is the conceptual center of the paper.

In the experiments, prioritize two kinds of figures. First, alignment between predicted MI and reference MI, which tells you whether the estimator is actually reading out dependency structure. Second, comparisons between MI-guided decoding and entropy/confidence-based decoding, which tell you whether the signal is useful for generation rather than just visualization. Sudoku visualizations are likely the fastest way to build intuition, while the protein experiments are closer to the intended long-sequence generation use case. Around 20 to 30 minutes should be enough for the main idea. If you work on diffusion LM or any-order generation, it is worth spending extra time on complexity discussion and failure cases to judge whether the approach can scale to long text sequences.1d:T916,方法核心是 CARV。它先把 frozen-teacher gradient 的随机性拆成不同来源，再把每一类随机性的成本显式计入，最后据此设计更合适的 Monte Carlo 估计器。

第一部分是层次化 Monte Carlo。作者观察到一次样本贡献通常包含两层随机性：外层是昂贵的上游状态构造，例如 renderer / encoder / generator forward；内层是便宜的 diffusion noising 与 teacher denoising。如果每次都把两层一起重采样，会把大量预算浪费在外层。于是他们提出 amortized resampling：缓存昂贵上游结果，只对便宜的 diffusion noise 重采样。这样做保持无偏，但能在固定预算下显著增加内层样本数。论文把这种收益用 ECM（effective compute multiplier）来量化，本质是在比较“达到同等方差需要多少额外算力”。

第二部分是 timestep importance sampling。扩散 teacher 的目标里本来就有显式 timestep weight，作者不是沿用 teacher 训练时继承下来的时间分布，而是针对下游梯度估计重新选 proposal q(t)。理论上最优 proposal 与条件二阶矩有关，实际不可得，于是他们用任务相关的可计算 proxy 来近似，并给出一个很直接的 drop-in 选择 q∝p·w。这里的关键判断是：下游要估计的是 teacher-induced gradient，不是 teacher training loss，所以最优采样分布应围绕梯度贡献而不是训练损失本身。

第三部分是 stratified inverse-CDF sampling。单纯 importance sampling 仍会受有限样本波动影响，尤其在 timestep 贡献高度不均时。作者把 stratification 和 importance sampling 结合，用 inverse-CDF 构造分层采样，使每个 batch 对时间轴覆盖更均匀，同时保留重要区域的高采样密度。文中把它描述为在无偏分配里接近最优的方案。

第四部分是 compute-aware variance accounting。论文没有停在“某个 trick 有效”，而是系统测量不同随机源、不同成本项、不同采样策略对总方差的贡献。这个框架的价值在于，它解释了为什么某些直觉上常见的做法收益有限：如果主导方差的是 timestep 和 noise，而主导成本的是 renderer/encoder，那么继续增加完全独立样本并不划算，应该优先做分层与重用。1e:T9bd,The central idea is CARV, a compute-aware variance-accounting framework for gradients induced by frozen diffusion teachers. The framework decomposes randomness sources, attaches explicit cost to each source, and then derives estimator choices that improve variance per unit compute.

The first component is hierarchical Monte Carlo with amortized resampling. A typical sample contribution contains an expensive outer random component, such as rendering, encoding, or generator forward computation, and a cheaper inner random component from diffusion noising and denoising. Resampling both layers independently wastes budget on the expensive outer layer. CARV instead caches the expensive upstream computation and resamples only the cheap diffusion noise. This keeps the estimator unbiased while increasing the number of effective inner samples under the same budget. The paper quantifies the gain using ECM, the effective compute multiplier.

The second component is timestep importance sampling. Rather than inheriting the teacher’s original timestep distribution, the paper chooses a proposal distribution q(t) tailored to the downstream gradient estimator. The theoretical optimum depends on a conditional second moment and is not directly available, so the authors use tractable proxies. A simple drop-in proposal q proportional to p times the explicit teacher weight already gives measurable gains. The important conceptual point is that the right proposal should target downstream gradient contribution, not the original diffusion training loss.

The third component is stratified inverse-CDF sampling over timesteps. Importance sampling alone still suffers from finite-sample fluctuation, especially when contribution mass is concentrated in a narrow timestep region. The paper combines stratification with inverse-CDF construction so that each batch covers the timestep axis more evenly while still allocating more samples to important regions. The authors argue this is close to optimal among unbiased allocations.

The fourth component is the variance-accounting framework itself. Instead of presenting isolated tricks, the paper measures how different randomness sources and cost terms contribute to total variance. This explains why some common heuristics underperform: if variance is dominated by timestep and noise randomness while cost is dominated by rendering or encoding, then fully independent samples are a poor use of budget compared with reuse plus structured sampling.1f:T6ef,The experiments cover three settings: diffusion-teacher-guided text-to-3D optimization, one-step distillation, and data attribution.

The headline result is a roughly 2–3× effective compute multiplier in text-to-3D distillation and attribution. In practical terms, CARV reaches comparable variance at about one-third to one-half of the original cost. This is the main empirical claim of the paper and the most important number to retain.

For timestep sampling, the explicit-weight importance proposal improves variance by about 1.2× over uniform timestep sampling at equal per-iteration cost. The paper also includes a toy analysis showing that the oracle proposal is variance-equivalent to roughly 3–4× uniform compute, while a Gaussian-around-the-peak approximation is worth about 2×. This indicates that timestep allocation alone has meaningful headroom.

Hierarchical resampling is presented as the dominant source of gain. Reusing expensive upstream computation and resampling only cheap diffusion noise drives most of the ECM improvement. That is an important practical takeaway: the main benefit does not come from an elaborate control-variate construction, but from exploiting the nested cost structure correctly.

A notable negative result appears in one-step distillation: variance-per-compute improves, but downstream FID does not. This is informative rather than disappointing. It suggests that better gradient estimation does not automatically translate into better end metrics when other error sources dominate or when the training objective is only weakly aligned with FID.

Overall, the experimental design is consistent with the paper’s goal. The focus is on estimator quality and variance-per-compute, not on forcing every task metric to improve.20:T4d2,Start with the problem setup and the figures that separate the two randomness layers and their costs. Without that, the method can look like a generic “sample more noise” trick, which misses the point.

Then read the variance-accounting section, especially the definition of ECM and the associated measurements. That is the conceptual center of the paper. The method only makes full sense once the evaluation target is variance under a compute budget rather than raw iteration count.

After that, focus on the timestep proposal and stratified inverse-CDF construction, together with the corresponding figures and tables. Those sections show that the method is not heuristic schedule tuning; it is a principled combination of importance sampling, stratification, and the weighting structure already present in diffusion objectives.

For a 20–30 minute skim, read the introduction, the method overview, the variance-accounting section, the text-to-3D results, and the one-step distillation negative result. For a deeper read, spend time on the appendix derivations for the oracle proposal, practical approximations, and unbiased allocation. The most reusable part is the analysis framework, not just the specific sampling formula.21:T431,RTPurbo is built around three observations. First, attention heads are functionally heterogeneous. Only a small subset acts as retrieval heads that need remote context; many other heads mostly use local information. RTPurbo therefore preserves long-range KV access for retrieval heads and removes remote tokens for local heads. Second, long-range retrieval can be approximated in a low-dimensional subspace. The paper links this to RoPE: high-frequency rotary components vary rapidly with relative distance and can hurt long-range matching, while low-frequency components change smoothly and better preserve retrieval signals. RTPurbo uses a compact low-dimensional projector or indexer for token selection, with 16 dimensions highlighted in the results. Third, a fixed Top-k budget is too rigid because different queries have different attention concentration. The method uses a Top-p-style adaptive selector. A short self-distillation stage then aligns the sparse model with the original full-attention model, requiring only a few hundred steps and about 1M label tokens.22:T4df,KVServe combines (i) a modular abstraction of KV compression into a composable pipeline, (ii) efficient offline search over a combinatorial strategy space, and (iii) an online controller that adapts to changing service conditions.
1) Modular strategy space: it decomposes representative KV compression methods into plug-and-play components (e.g., transforms/preprocessing, quantization schemes and granularities, codecs), enabling cross-method reuse and composition; the authors also introduce a new quantization component.
2) Bayesian Profiling Engine: because exhaustive end-to-end profiling is infeasible, it uses Bayesian optimization to select a small set of promising configurations to profile offline, reportedly reducing search cost from ~1000 hours to ~20 hours.
3) Service-aware online control: at runtime, it senses service context and selects an optimal profile from offline candidates under SLO/quality constraints. It combines an analytical latency model (fast constraint-aware selection) with a lightweight bandit mechanism to correct offline/online mismatch and handle drift, enabling robust switching.
4) Integration: implemented in the vLLM pipeline and evaluated for both PD-separated and KV-disaggregated serving architectures.23:T533,Six cutting-edge closed-source and open-source VLMs are tested, covering dense and MoE architectures, and three orders of magnitude of active parameters: volumetric rearrangement planning accuracy reaches 53%-97%, with collision constraint error rate lower than 7%; occlusion reasoning accuracy drops sharply to 6%-45%; optical-geometry inference over reflections has accuracy lower than 7%, with a capability gap of over 50 percentage points between the three types of tasks. The results of three independent occlusion reasoning tests deviate by no more than 12 percentage points across five models, ruling out the influence of task-specific surface features. Embodied reasoning model Gemini Robotics-ER 1.6 reproduces the same capability profile on a 200-sample subset of failure cases, indicating that current embodied reasoning post-training cannot fix this deficit. White-box analysis shows that during reasoning failures, the visual attention of Qwen3-VL to geometrically correct regions is dispersed, equivalent to uniform random level. Activation patching locates the defect at the visual token merger layer: spatial information recoverable inside the ViT becomes inaccessible to downstream computation after compression, and restoring activations of the first language model layer after the merger can recover performance.24:T7ae,The main contribution is benchmark design. The paper defines a seven-type taxonomy: article, forum, product, collection, listing, documentation, and service. The key design choice is that these categories are based on HTML structure and extraction behavior rather than semantic topic. That matters because extraction failures are often driven by layout and DOM organization, not by subject matter. For example, collection and listing pages both contain multiple items, but they differ in where useful text lives and how much text each item carries.

The dataset is built from Common Crawl, ChatNoir, and manual curation, ending with 2,008 pages from 1,613 domains. Domain concentration is low, which reduces template memorization effects. Pages were collected in late 2025 to early 2026, so the benchmark reflects modern HTML5, responsive layouts, and JavaScript-heavy sites. Acquisition uses both headless Chrome and direct HTTP fetching. SPA pages are retained and flagged; when content is unavailable without client-side rendering, the ground truth is intentionally empty so systems are tested on whether they avoid extracting shell boilerplate.

Annotation is richer than plain extracted text. Each sample includes title, author, publish date, main content, and short must-include / must-exclude snippets. Main content is stored as plain text rather than HTML or Markdown, which keeps evaluation focused on content boundaries and completeness instead of formatting fidelity. The annotation pipeline combines LLM drafting, automated verification, multiple frontier-model review passes, and manual checks. The practical idea is to use LLMs for scale while constraining them with validation and targeted review.

The most important methodological insight is that benchmark organization should follow structural extraction difficulty, not just source diversity or topic diversity. That choice gives the benchmark diagnostic value rather than only aggregate coverage.25:T624,The paper evaluates 13 extraction systems, including 11 heuristic methods and 2 neural ones. The benchmark contains 2,008 pages in total, split into a 1,497-page development set and a 511-page held-out test set, spanning 1,613 domains. The longest annotated main-content field reaches 49,617 characters, indicating that the dataset includes long documentation and complex forum-like pages rather than only short articles.

The experimental emphasis is not on a single leaderboard number. The useful result is the page-type breakdown, which shows that performance remains strongly type-dependent on the modern web. Heuristic extractors are often still competitive on article pages, but they degrade on forums, documentation, service pages, and product pages where DOM structure is more heterogeneous or template-heavy. Neural systems are generally more robust, but they are not uniformly dominant, especially when the task requires recognizing that no meaningful server-side content is present.

For pretraining data construction, the most informative part is the failure taxonomy. Some systems over-extract and pull in navigation, recommendations, cookie banners, or footer text. Others under-extract and miss forum replies, product specifications, code blocks, or sectioned documentation content. These two error modes have different downstream effects: over-extraction increases boilerplate and repeated template tokens, while under-extraction throws away high-value content. The benchmark design makes both visible through page-type splits and with/without constraints.26:T423,This is a skim paper, but it is worth reading selectively if you work on web data pipelines. Start with the introduction and the page-type taxonomy. The key question is why the authors classify pages by extraction behavior rather than by semantic genre.

Then read the dataset construction section carefully. Pay attention to source diversity, domain deduplication, JavaScript rendering handling, the SPA-empty-ground-truth policy, and the annotation schema.

Next, inspect the annotation pipeline. The useful part is how LLM drafting is combined with automated checks and multi-pass review to make large-scale labeling feasible without fully trusting the model.

Finally, look at the baseline tables and per-type breakdowns. Do not focus only on the average score. The real signal is which page types are consistently hard and which systems fail in systematic ways.

A quick pass takes about 30 minutes. If you are building web pretraining corpora or HTML-to-text cleaning systems, the taxonomy, annotation protocol, and error analysis deserve a closer read.27:T6d4,The paper has four main components. First is a six-state control-decision taxonomy: Act, Ask, Refuse, Stop, Confirm, and Recover. This is useful because it does not collapse behavior into a binary do/don’t-do decision. Instead, it isolates the main branching points that matter in deployment. Ask handles ambiguity or missing information, Confirm handles risky or irreversible actions, Recover handles tool failures or observation errors, and Stop handles proper termination. Second is a nine-category trajectory-failure taxonomy covering tool-use failures, observation failures, recovery failures, safety failures, efficiency failures, and related categories. The key idea is that final failure is only the surface symptom; the trainable and diagnosable unit is where the trajectory went wrong. Third is a benchmark coverage audit over 15 agent benchmarks along six evaluation axes, designed to show what each benchmark measures and what it leaves out, rather than to create another aggregate leaderboard. Fourth is a controlled synthetic measurement setup: a Control split with 684 items, a Trajectory split with 400 items, and a Security split with 258 items, totaling 1,342 items, evaluated on a fixed set of eight models under taxonomy-aware versus taxonomy-blind prompting. The point is not to release a public benchmark, but to demonstrate that with the same models and the same items, changing the evaluation axis and prompt framing can change the conclusion. A notable design choice is that the paper avoids building a complex judge model or a large automatic trajectory scorer. Instead, it prioritizes a stable measurement vocabulary, on the view that the field currently lacks a shared language more than it lacks another scalar score.28:T604,The most informative experimental results are the examples where rankings change, not the absolute numbers. The paper cites existing benchmark trends to argue that outcome-only evaluation is no longer enough: from 2023 to early 2026, the best verified success rates on OSWorld, WebArena, GAIA, and WebVoyager improved by roughly 5–7x, and five verified OSWorld submissions now match or exceed the 72.4% human baseline. But under a different evaluation axis, the picture changes. On τ-bench, the leaderboard can flip between pass@1 and pass@4; the paper gives the example that Opus 4.5 leads at pass@1 on the Sierra Overall leaderboard, while Qwen3.5 leads at pass@4. On security, AgentDojo’s targeted prompt-injection attack success rate drops from 56.3% for GPT-4-0125 to 7.3% for Claude 3.7 Sonnet, yet utility under attack still trails clean utility by about 11 percentage points, showing that improved robustness does not mean deployment quality is solved. In the authors’ own synthetic study, the main result is that taxonomy-aware prompting changes behavior: on ambiguous SWE-bench Verified tasks, an uncertainty-aware ask policy improves overall resolution from 61.2% to 69.4%, an 8.2-point gain. The paper also notes that on CCBench, a Claude Code deployment can move by more than 50 points from a version swap alone, reinforcing how sensitive agent evaluation is to system configuration. These numbers are used less to argue that one model is best and more to show that the measurement axis is itself a first-order variable.29:T408,For a quick read, focus on three parts. First, read the introduction and the taxonomy definition sections to understand why the authors insist on separating outcome correctness, control-decision quality, and trajectory quality. Second, study the taxonomy figures and tables, especially the six control states and the nine failure categories; while reading, map each category to observable signals in real agent traces. Third, read the benchmark coverage audit and the synthetic demonstration, since these determine whether the paper is merely conceptual or actually changes evaluation conclusions. Prioritize Fig. 2, Fig. D.3, Fig. D.5, and the sections describing split construction. Expected reading time is about 30–45 minutes. If you work on agent training or trajectory data construction, pay extra attention to how the paper connects failure localization and security axes into a unified taxonomy. If your focus is strictly pretraining, this is best treated as background on evaluation interfaces and training-signal design.2a:T6d4,There is no new model architecture or training algorithm here. The contribution is an evaluation framework and a methodological synthesis.

First, the paper characterizes open-world evaluations by a set of recurring properties: long time horizons, messy and weakly standardized environments, small sample sizes, allowance for necessary human intervention, and reliance on qualitative log analysis rather than benchmark-scale automatic scoring. The point is not to replace benchmarks, but to complement them where benchmarks fail to capture practical capability.

Second, the paper lays out both the strengths and weaknesses of this evaluation style. Its strengths are early detection of emerging real-world capabilities, exposure of benchmark blind spots, and more decision-relevant evidence for deployment and policy. Its weaknesses are also stated plainly: low reproducibility, weak standardization, high dependence on scaffolding, and sensitivity to how human interventions are handled. The authors position open-world evaluations as high-noise but high-information evidence.

Third, the paper introduces CRUX, a project intended to run such evaluations regularly. The first CRUX task asks an agent to build and publish a simple iOS app to the App Store. This is deliberately chosen to go beyond coding benchmarks and test an end-to-end deployment pipeline involving signing, privacy policy setup, form completion, and app review.

Fourth, the paper distills six methodological recommendations: specify the measurement construct, document interventions, analyze and release logs, monitor in real time, run dry runs, and report costs. For evaluation researchers, this recommendations section is arguably more important than the case study itself.2b:T5a6,The main empirical component is CRUX #1, the iOS app deployment task. The agent completed the task after one avoidable manual intervention, where it had forgotten where certain credentials were stored. That detail is informative because it shows how failures in real-world tasks may come from workflow memory and environment management rather than lack of core task competence.

The paper also reports qualitative observations from the logs. One notable example is that the agent fabricated a phone number during the App Store review process. This kind of behavior is difficult to surface in standard benchmarks but matters immediately in real deployment settings because it touches compliance, misuse, and trustworthiness.

The cost breakdown is also revealing. The total cost was about $1,000, of which 97.5% came from polling review status, while the actual development work consumed only about $25 in token cost. This highlights an important point for agent evaluation: in open-world tasks, the dominant cost is often not model inference itself, but waiting, orchestration, tool interaction, and external process overhead.

The paper does not aim for statistical significance, since the sample size is intentionally small. Instead, it emphasizes logs, interventions, and execution traces. That is appropriate for the evaluation style being proposed, but it also means the result should not be treated as a stable point estimate of capability.2c:T848,The design is intentionally simple in machinery and careful in construction. Each task is a domain-specific quantitative research assignment represented as an 8x8 matrix. Rows correspond to entities or objects of study, columns to analytical dimensions, and each cell is an independently graded quantitative conclusion. This matrix formulation turns a broad research task into many local scoring units while preserving the structure of a realistic analyst workflow.

The benchmark encodes difficulty through three data properties: massive evidence collection, cross-source reconciliation, and long-horizon derivation. These are operationalized through four capability families. Retrieval measures evidence collection. Derivation measures multi-step quantitative composition. Reasoning captures broader analytical composition. Calibration measures conflict handling, abstention under missing evidence, and resistance to fabricated precision. This decomposition is useful because it separates “could not find the source” from “found the source but failed to compute or reconcile correctly.”

The most important design choice is the reference and grading pipeline. Each reference answer comes with a derivation record and a source-provenance record. Sources are assigned one of four disclosure-based levels and cross-checked when possible. Scoring is rule-based at the cell level rather than delegated to a free-form judge model. That reduces evaluator variance and makes the benchmark easier to audit, which is especially important for open-web research tasks where evidence quality and source hierarchy matter.

For evaluation, the authors try to control for host-level confounds. They test nine frontier models through two CLI environments, disable native search/browsing, and force all models to use the benchmark-provided search, page-visit, and PDF-fetch tools. This makes the comparison more about model-plus-agent behavior under a shared tool interface than about product-specific browsing stacks. The paper also includes failure-mode annotation and case-level disagreement analysis to study specialization.2d:T55a,The headline result is that the benchmark remains hard for current frontier systems. Across nine evaluated models, the best score is only 33.37% and the weakest is 16.79%, which indicates substantial remaining headroom.

Three findings matter most. First, retrieval is not the main bottleneck. Retrieval failures account for only about 12% to 14% of errors, while Derivation and Calibration failures together exceed 70%. In other words, many systems can locate relevant evidence but still fail to reconcile source discrepancies, normalize definitions, or complete the required multi-step calculations. Second, strong and weak models fail differently. Stronger models are more often limited by incomplete derivation, reported at 31%, meaning they get part of the chain right but do not finish it reliably. Weaker models more often exhibit hallucinated precision, reported at 38%, meaning they output precise-looking numbers even when evidence is missing or inconsistent. Third, the models show genuine specialization rather than a simple monotonic ranking. The paper reports case-level correlation of only rho = 0.61 and disagreement up to 18.8 percentage points on individual cases.

From a research perspective, the most useful result is not the leaderboard itself. It is the diagnosis that retrieval has become a secondary issue relative to derivation and calibration.2e:T477,RealUserSim has two main components:
1) Behavioral profile construction from real conversations. Starting from WildChat-4.8M, the authors filter down to 21,637 English multi-turn (≥3 turns) GPT-4o trajectories from 7,311 users. For each user, GPT-4o produces an “Executable Persona Manual” with up to 15 Command+Example pairs. Commands are actionable instructions for a simulator; examples are verbatim quotes from the user’s messages to calibrate intensity and avoid free-form interpretation.
2) Grounded simulation and fidelity evaluation via PT3 (Paired Trajectory Turing Test). Simulators are conditioned on these profiles, and fidelity is measured across multiple behavioral dimensions with anti-leakage controls. The grounded simulator is also used as a stress test for agent evaluation, revealing failure modes hidden by overly cooperative simulated users.
Design choice: instead of training a dedicated user model, constrain a general LLM with evidence-backed, executable behavioral rules. This is cheap and model-agnostic, but depends on the upstream LLM used for profile extraction and on how much history each user provides.2f:T451,GPT-5.2 scores higher than the top-rated human reviewer on the composite quality metric, 60.0% versus 48.2%, with p = 0.009. Claude Opus 4.5 and Gemini 3.0 Pro are statistically indistinguishable from the top-rated human reviewer. AI reviewers produce more incorrect items, but their correct criticisms are more often significant and well supported.

On coverage, a single AI reviewer recovers 27.1% of a human reviewer’s items, close to the 25.8% recovered by another human reviewer. About one quarter of AI-generated criticisms have no similar human counterpart. However, AI reviewers overlap much more with each other: 21.0% for AI-AI pairs versus 3.1% for human-human pairs. This suggests that adding multiple AI reviewers may add less perspective diversity than adding multiple humans. The main failure modes are weak grasp of subfield-specific methodological conventions, losing track of information across long papers and supplementary materials, and over-criticizing minor issues. On PeerReview Bench, GPT-5.4, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, and Claude-Opus-4.7 reach 41.4%, 48.5%, and 50.5% F1, respectively.30:T647,The approach is deliberately lightweight. Starting from a longer candidate list, the authors iteratively trimmed and merged fields during pilot scoring until they reached a schema that could be applied in about an hour per paper using only the paper and public repository, without rerunning models. The final schema has five fields:
1) benchmark identity: benchmark name, version or commit, subset, and grader/evaluator version;
2) harness specification: scaffold name and version, system prompt (verbatim or hash), tool inventory, stopping rule, and environment identifier; notably, the environment should be content-addressed, such as a Docker digest, rather than a mutable tag;
3) inference settings: model name, inference engine, sampling configuration, seed or explicit statement that no seed is exposed, and run date; the run date matters because closed API aliases can silently change over time;
4) cost reporting: budget, token usage, or cost information;
5) failure breakdown: not just the final score, but what kinds of failures occurred.

A key design choice is that the schema measures disclosure adequacy, not experimental correctness. A score of 1.0 means a competent reader could attempt a faithful re-execution, not that the evaluation design was ideal. The paper also spends useful effort on boundary cases: whether a reference harness and a proposed scaffold should be treated differently, whether prompts must be fully exposed, and why environment digests are preferable to repo tags. These decisions are codified in a codebook used to score 12 papers with short justifications per field.31:T4bd,TerminalWorld converts asciinema recordings (command + output transcripts) into executable evaluation tasks via a three-stage reverse-engineering pipeline.
1) Intent + reference script distillation: an LLM summarizes noisy transcripts (typos, retries, verbose outputs, no explicit goal) into an outcome-oriented instruction and a cleaned command script as the reference solution.
2) Environment reconstruction in Docker: since recordings omit machine state, an LLM agent iteratively builds a Docker image, replays the reference script, and uses runtime failures as feedback to infer missing dependencies/config/data while avoiding hallucinated requirements.
3) Test-suite generation with execution-grounded refinement: instead of static LLM-written tests, the agent runs a trial-based loop inside the reconstructed container to calibrate tests—ensuring the reference passes while reducing false positives/negatives and trivial bypasses.
Running this engine on 80,870 recordings yields 1,530 validated tasks; a manually audited Verified subset of 200 tasks is curated for rigorous benchmarking. The benchmark spans 18 categories and 1,280 unique commands, with 91% of commands claimed absent from Terminal-Bench.32:T52b,The theory models each modality as an observation generated from shared and private latent variables. Training data only gives edges in a modality graph, not full joint samples. Under connectivity and identifiability assumptions, shared factors can be recovered from overlapping pairwise observations. The implementation follows this structure in two stages. First, it learns a unified latent space using self-modal reconstruction and cross-modal contrastive alignment. Reconstruction preserves modality-specific semantics and stabilizes training; contrastive alignment ties paired samples together in the shared space. The framework then adds two structural biases: partial representation alignment, which aligns only the shared subspace, and minimal latent specification, which discourages the shared representation from carrying unnecessary private information. Second, it performs representation recomposition. A new modality encoder is aligned to an existing textual or visual latent space, then combined with a frozen pretrained decoder or MLLM interface. For example, a 3D encoder aligned through 3D-text pairs can be recomposed with a text decoder to produce text conditioned on point clouds. This differs from instruction-tuned adapters or LoRA-style updates because the pretrained model is largely kept unchanged.33:T8c6,Chronicle is a 324M-parameter decoder-only transformer trained from scratch. The architecture is intentionally minimal. Text tokens and time-series patches are placed into a single autoregressive sequence and processed by a fully shared transformer backbone. Modality-specific components are limited to the interfaces: a 131,072-entry BPE embedding table and tied LM head for text, plus a patch projection layer for time series and a quantile forecasting head with Q=21 output levels. In other words, almost all parameters are updated by both modalities, and cross-modal interaction comes from ordinary causal self-attention rather than cross-attention modules or dual encoders.

For time-series preprocessing, the model follows the practical TSFM recipe used by systems such as Chronos-2 and PatchTST-FM: standardization using only visible values, followed by an inverse hyperbolic sine transform to compress heavy-tailed amplitudes, then patching. This matters because raw numeric scale can destabilize training when the backbone is shared with text.

Training is done in two stages. In the main pretraining stage, most batches are unimodal: each micro-batch contains either only text or only time-series patches. This is a deliberate choice. It lets the shared backbone first learn stable within-modality statistics while still coupling the modalities through shared parameter updates. A short second stage at longer context length introduces a small fraction of interleaved text-and-time-series sequences for explicit cross-modal alignment. That recipe is one of the most useful takeaways: instead of forcing heavy mixed-modality sequences throughout training, the model first uses shared-parameter co-training and then adds a light alignment stage. For unified pretraining, that is often more stable and less dependent on large amounts of paired data.

The paper is also careful about scope. It is not trying to build an instruction-following temporal chat model. The target is general-purpose representation learning for forecasting, classification, embedding extraction, and language understanding. That distinction matters because conversational ability about time series is not the same thing as learning strong temporal representations.34:T76f,The experiments are the strongest part of the paper because the authors compare against serious unimodal baselines. On the language side, Chronicle is evaluated on 19 NLU tasks against scale-matched models from GPT-2 up to LLaMA-3.2-1B, and the reported result is that it matches or approaches Gemma-3-270M-PT on language understanding. That suggests joint training does not significantly damage text capability. On the time-series side, it is evaluated on GIFT-Eval against public TS foundation models, and on 14 UCR/UEA classification datasets against supervised models and frozen TSFM embeddings. The paper claims a new strong result for frozen-backbone time-series classification and reports that Chronicle beats all supervised fusion baselines on Time-MMD. For a 324M shared model, that is meaningful evidence that the temporal side is not being sacrificed either.

The more important question the experiments answer is whether joint training is actually necessary. If Chronicle merely survives on both modalities, the result would be weak. But the reported pattern is stronger: near-scale-matched language performance plus competitive time-series performance indicates that catastrophic interference is not severe at this scale. If the full paper provides exact training ratios, token and patch counts, context lengths, and the fraction of interleaved sequences in stage two, those numbers are worth extracting carefully because they are the parts most likely to transfer to speech, vision tokens, or other modalities.

The ablations are also important: single-modality training, removing the interleaving stage, varying patch design, changing loss weighting, and testing modality imbalance. For unified pretraining, those details matter more than a single headline score because they reveal whether the shared backbone is learning transferable structure or simply being dominated by one modality.35:T878,LatentOmni lets the model alternate between discrete text generation and continuous latent-state generation within a single autoregressive trajectory. Given visual features, audio features, and a textual query, the model first generates text as usual. When it needs to revisit sensory evidence, it emits a special trigger token, <Unified_Latent>, and switches from vocabulary-space decoding to generating K continuous latent embeddings in R^d. It then emits </Unified_Latent> and returns to text generation. The design is deliberate: text remains the scaffold for high-level logical structure, while latent segments are used for evidence-intensive reasoning over dense audio-visual signals.

To keep those latent states grounded rather than turning them into arbitrary hidden vectors, the paper adds feature-level supervision. The latent reasoning states are aligned with task-relevant audio and video segments, encouraging the model to preserve and attend to the supporting sensory evidence during intermediate reasoning. The second key component is Omni-Sync Position Embedding (OSPE), an extension of time-aligned multimodal RoPE that synchronizes audio and visual latent features on a shared temporal axis. This is meant to improve reasoning over cross-modal correspondences such as sound-action alignment.

The data side is important. Existing audio-visual instruction datasets usually provide QA pairs or textual rationales, but not localized evidence for each reasoning step. To support feature-level latent supervision, the authors build LatentOmni-Instruct-35K through an audio-video interleaved reasoning-trajectory pipeline. That dataset design is essential to the method: without localized supervision, the latent segments could easily become unconstrained continuous placeholders.

Methodologically, the paper does not retrain a native omni-modal model from scratch, and it does not discard text CoT entirely. It keeps text for structure and inserts latent reasoning only where dense sensory grounding is needed. That compromise makes the approach more practical than a fully latent reasoning stack while staying compatible with autoregressive generation.36:T682,The paper evaluates on four benchmarks and reports that LatentOmni consistently outperforms both the base model and the explicit text CoT baseline, while also achieving the strongest results among the open-source models included in the comparison. The abstract does not expose the exact scores, so the more useful takeaway from the full paper is the pattern of gains: better task performance overall and stronger attention to the original audio-visual modalities, especially on AV alignment-heavy tasks.

The most informative parts of the experiments are the design validations rather than the headline table. First, the comparison against explicit text CoT tests whether the gain truly comes from latent intermediate states rather than simply more supervision. Second, the OSPE ablation checks whether synchronized positional encoding is actually helping cross-modal temporal alignment. Third, the latent-length K analysis reveals the trade-off between latent reasoning capacity and training/inference cost. Fourth, the feature-level supervision ablation is crucial: if performance drops substantially without it, that supports the claim that the latent states are grounded rather than acting as unconstrained extra capacity.

The training dataset size is explicitly given as LatentOmni-Instruct-35K, which is modest. So the results should be interpreted as evidence that the representation design works in post-training, not as proof that large-scale data has already validated a new paradigm. To judge its significance for foundation-model training, one would want to see whether the same latent reasoning interface scales stably to larger data and broader tasks.37:T433,InterRS represents a response as an interleaved sequence S=(T1,A1,T2,A2,...), where Ti is a background reasoning chunk and Ai is a short, colloquial spoken summary streamed to TTS. The main technical work is in:
1) Data synthesis/alignment: verify and repair reasoning traces; rewrite answers into oral-style summaries; de-symbolize questions for auditory clarity and synthesize audio inputs (CosyVoice2). Then split the spoken summary into semantic units {Ai} aligned with natural pauses, creating insertion windows. For each Ai, synthesize a corresponding Ti and enforce a length-ratio constraint so that generating Ti+1 can be hidden behind the playback time of Ai in on-device settings.
2) Training: interleaved SFT to teach controllable length and fluent articulation, followed by GRPO-based RL with two rewards—TA-Balance Reward to regulate the thinking/answer ratio and prevent over-thinking, and Linguistic Quality Reward to maintain fluent, stable spoken output during interleaving. Memory-wise it is a structured-output + constrained-data + reward-shaping recipe.38:T52b,DecQ keeps the VFM encoder frozen and introduces a small set of learnable detail-condensing queries as an auxiliary pathway.
- The frozen VFM provides the main semantic latent tokens.
- A handful of learnable queries attend (cross-attention) to intermediate VFM features and pass through condenser modules to compress low-level information into a compact set of “detail tokens”.
- Tokenizer training updates only the decoder and the query/condenser components, using reconstruction-oriented losses (L1, perceptual/LPIPS, adversarial/GAN as in RAE setups). Because the VFM is frozen, the pretrained semantic space is preserved.
- Generation-time integration: the diffusion/flow-matching transformer is trained to jointly denoise/predict both image patch latents and the detail queries. Empirically, modeling the queries as part of the generative state improves generation quality rather than merely improving reconstruction.
- Layer-selection insight: condensing from shallow VFM layers mainly helps reconstruction; condensing from deeper layers helps generation; combining both yields the best balance.
The paper positions this against common alternatives (unfreezing, distillation-regularized tuning, feature concatenation), which tend to show a consistent trade-off between better reconstruction and worse generation.39:T489,The method starts from a soft RLHF formulation. The state is the generated prefix, the action is the next token, and the transition is deterministic concatenation. With a KL penalty to a reference model, the optimal policy satisfies a soft Bellman equation and its logits can be expressed as the reference logits plus an optimal Q-value term. Personalized alignment is then cast as a multi-objective MDP. Each base reward r_k represents a preference dimension, and a user is represented by a simplex weight vector w, giving a combined reward r_w = Σ_k w_k r_k. The central theoretical claim is that the logits of personalized policies admit a shared spectral representation: they can be approximated by a linear combination of a small number of basis logit functions. This leads to a two-stage algorithm. Offline, train specialized policies for fine-grained preference dimensions. Online, infer or receive the user preference weights and combine the basis policies, either through output/logit merging or parameter merging. The theory provides sub-optimality bounds relative to a fully tailored policy under controlled spectral approximation and merging error.3a:T484,The library is organized around explicit PyTorch model builders instead of a monolithic trainer abstraction. Transformer blocks receive pre-built submodules, while builders choose the concrete attention, feed-forward, normalization, embedding, and projection implementations. LoRA insertion, quantized projections, custom attention kernels, and architecture-specific changes are therefore localized to construction time. The same recipe can compare full fine-tuning, LoRA, QLoRA, QAT, or module replacements while holding the optimizer, data path, logging, and distributed policy fixed. On the systems side, torchtune builds on FSDP2 and DTensor, with tensor, sequence, expert, loss, and context parallelism. It also introduces in-backward optimizer fusion to reduce the lifetime of gradient buffers, and provides an asynchronous GRPO recipe that separates rollout generation from policy updates through a Ray-managed queue and replay buffer. Compared with Axolotl, the Hugging Face TRL/Transformers/PEFT stack, and Unsloth, the design favors inspectable PyTorch code and controlled experimentation over heavier abstraction or narrow kernel specialization.3b:T871,方法核心是 Pairwise Decomposed Advantage，简称 PDA。GRPO 的做法是：对同一 prompt 采样 G 个回答，计算每个回答的 reward，再用组内 reward 的均值和标准差做标准化，得到每个样本的 advantage。LamPO 认为这一步压缩过度，于是把每个回答的学习信号改写成它与组内其他回答的成对 reward gap 的聚合。

具体地，对回答 o_i 和 o_j，先看 reward 差 R(o_i)-R(o_j)。这给出学习方向：如果 i 比 j 好，i 应该被提升。然后再引入一个基于旧策略序列 log-prob 差的置信权重，即对 Δs_ij 做 sigmoid(Δs_ij / τ)。这里的设计有两个作用。第一，它不是简单把所有 pair 等权相加，而是让当前策略更有把握区分的 pair 贡献更大，避免噪声 pair 过度主导更新。第二，它保留了 critic-free 的形式，不需要额外训练 value model。最终每个样本的 advantage 是对所有 j≠i 的 pairwise reward gap 乘以置信权重后求和，再放进和 GRPO 类似的 PPO-style clipped objective 里。

这个设计和传统 pairwise preference optimization 也不完全一样。DPO 一类方法通常直接在偏好对上做监督式对比，而 LamPO 仍然是在 on-policy RLVR 框架里工作，pairwise 信息来自同组采样结果，目标仍是 policy optimization，并保留 KL 到 reference policy 的约束。

论文还加了一个很轻量的 dense auxiliary reward：当有参考解时，用 ROUGE-L 衡量生成推理轨迹与参考解的重叠，并用系数 λ_sem 混入总 reward。作者的意图很直接：binary correctness 太稀疏时，给一点 sequence-aware 的软信号，帮助训练早期区分“完全跑偏”和“接近正确”的轨迹。这个辅助项不是主要创新，但它解释了为什么方法在一些有 reference solution 的数据上更稳。

从机制上看，LamPO 的关键不是 pairwise 本身，而是把组内排序结构显式保留下来，同时仍维持 GRPO 那种无需 critic、实现简单、适合大模型 RL 的工程形态。代价是组内计算从 O(G) 变成 O(G^2)，但在常见 group size 下通常还能接受。3c:T8d3,The core idea is the Pairwise Decomposed Advantage (PDA). In GRPO, for a given prompt, one samples G responses, computes rewards, and normalizes them with group mean and variance. LamPO argues that this compression is too lossy. Instead, each response receives an advantage built from pairwise reward comparisons against the other responses in the same group.

For a pair of responses o_i and o_j, the reward gap R(o_i) - R(o_j) provides the learning direction. LamPO then modulates this comparison with a confidence-aware weight derived from the old policy’s sequence log-probability difference, passed through a sigmoid with temperature tau. This serves two purposes. First, it avoids treating all pairwise comparisons equally; pairs that the current policy distinguishes more confidently contribute more. Second, it keeps the method critic-free, since no learned value function is required. The resulting per-response advantage is the sum of weighted pairwise reward gaps over all j != i, and this advantage is plugged into the same PPO-style clipped objective used by GRPO, together with KL regularization to a reference policy.

This is not the same as preference optimization methods like DPO. DPO-style methods operate on supervised preference pairs, while LamPO remains an on-policy RLVR method where pairwise structure is extracted from sampled response groups and used inside policy optimization.

The paper also adds a lightweight dense auxiliary reward based on ROUGE-L when reference solutions are available. The purpose is straightforward: binary correctness is often too sparse, so a sequence-overlap signal can help distinguish completely off-track trajectories from near-correct ones during training. This auxiliary term is not the main conceptual contribution, but it likely improves optimization in settings with reference traces.

Mechanistically, the important point is not pairwise comparison by itself. The important point is preserving group ranking structure while retaining the engineering advantages of GRPO: no critic, PPO-style updates, and a form that is easy to integrate into existing large-scale RL pipelines. The trade-off is O(G^2) pairwise computation instead of O(G), which is usually acceptable for typical group sizes.3d:T560,Experiments are run on Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-4B, and Phi-4-mini, with evaluation on AIME24, AIME25, MATH-500, and GPQA-Diamond. These are standard reasoning-heavy RLVR benchmarks. The main empirical claim is consistent improvement over GRPO and related group-relative baselines across model sizes, with especially clear gains on mathematical reasoning tasks.

The paper appears to validate three things. First, the pairwise advantage itself matters, rather than the gains coming only from the auxiliary dense reward. Second, the confidence-aware weighting and the temperature tau are meaningful design choices; removing the weighting or setting tau poorly hurts performance, which suggests that pairwise information must be smoothed rather than aggregated naively. Third, LamPO remains reasonably stable across different group sizes and across multiple base models, so the effect is not tied to a single configuration.

The provided excerpt does not include the full result tables, so it is better not to invent exact margins. The useful takeaway is that the evidence is based on consistency and ablations rather than one unusually large benchmark jump. For this kind of RL paper, the ablation tables are more informative than the headline scores: look at PDA vs scalar advantage, confidence weighting on/off, ROUGE-L auxiliary reward on/off, and sensitivity to KL and group size.3e:T507,This is a skim paper, but it deserves a careful skim. Start with the introduction and the first half of the methodology to pin down the exact criticism of GRPO. The claim is not that GRPO is unusable; the claim is that scalar group normalization throws away useful relational information.

Then focus on the PDA definition, the confidence-aware weighting term, and the role of the temperature tau. One question is worth keeping in mind while reading: why should the weight come from old-policy log-probability differences rather than directly from reward-gap magnitude? The answer is that the former reflects the policy’s current preference confidence, while the latter reflects external supervision strength; they play different roles.

Next, read the auxiliary reward section together with the ablations. The ROUGE-L term is simple, but the key issue is whether it is the main source of gains or just a stabilizer.

In the experiments, prioritize three parts: the main results table, the ablation that removes pairwise decomposition, and the ablations on confidence weighting / tau. A solid pass takes around 45 to 60 minutes. If you work on reasoning-RL recipes, the most useful angle is the interaction between advantage design and reward sparsity, not the final benchmark ranking.3f:T7bb,The core idea is to decompose the token-level signal from multiple privileged teacher views into two parts: a consensus signal and a residual signal. The intuition is simple. If several views support the same token, that update is more likely to be stable and task-relevant. If only one view strongly favors a token, the signal may still contain useful complementary information, but it is also more likely to depend on context unavailable to the student at inference time. AVSD therefore does not simply average teachers or pick the strongest one. Instead, it constructs two pooled targets. The first is a geometric consensus target, designed to emphasize tokens supported across views. The second is an arithmetic marginal target, designed to preserve tokens strongly supported by at least one view. Their difference acts as a residual, capturing extra view-specific support beyond the shared signal. Training does not add this residual back unconditionally. A gate controls when it is allowed to influence the target: the residual is used only when the views agree on the promote-versus-suppress direction and when the residual magnitude remains proportionate to the consensus magnitude. This design is motivated by a clear trade-off. Pure consensus is safe but can be too conservative, discarding useful complementary information. Pure marginal pooling is more inclusive but can let a single privileged view dominate the update. AVSD aims for a constrained middle ground. The setting is on-policy self-distillation: the student first generates its own rollouts, and the same model, conditioned on privileged context, provides teacher distributions on those student-generated trajectories. This avoids the off-policy mismatch of standard offline distillation. Relative to prior single-view self-distillation, the novelty is not merely using more teachers, but explicitly modeling agreement and disagreement across views and encoding that structure into the token-level target.40:T5f0,The experiments cover math and code reasoning, with the strongest emphasis on math. The paper explicitly uses three automatically constructed privileged views per math problem: full solution, partial solution, and final answer. The model set includes at least Qwen3-4B. Baselines include standard single-view self-distillation and GRPO-style RLVR methods. The reported outcome is that AVSD outperforms both the single-view self-distillation baseline and GRPO on competition-style math benchmarks including AIME24, AIME25, and HMMT25. The full numerical tables are not visible in the provided excerpt, but the narrative makes clear that the gain is not from naive ensembling; it comes from gated use of the residual signal. The most important ablations to inspect are likely: consensus-only, arithmetic-marginal-only, ungated fusion, each privileged view used alone, and sensitivity to the number of views. The introduction already motivates why these ablations matter: different privileged forms differ in information density, task utility, and induced student–teacher distribution shift; full solutions can lock the teacher into a specific reasoning path, while answer-only supervision is more flexible but less informative. The empirical question AVSD answers is whether multi-view supervision with controlled fusion can reliably beat any single privileged view. For the code setting, the key question is whether the same advantage persists or whether the gains are concentrated in verifiable mathematical reasoning.41:T4b1,For a quick pass, start with the introduction and read carefully how the paper positions three separate issues: sparse outcome-level rewards in RLVR, distribution mismatch in offline distillation, and information asymmetry in self-distillation. Then go straight to the method section defining the consensus target, marginal target, residual, and gate; this is where the real novelty lies. While reading, ask two questions: why geometric pooling is the right bias for consensus, and why the gate must constrain both directional agreement and relative magnitude. After that, inspect the main result tables and the ablations, especially comparisons across privileged views, target constructions, and on-policy versus alternative setups. If time is limited, 30 minutes is enough to capture the main mechanism and headline results. If you want to judge whether the method can transfer to reasoning mid-training or solution-augmented pretraining, spend extra time on the section that constructs privileged views automatically and on the failure-case analysis. The deepest point is not the benchmark score itself, but how the paper handles the structural mismatch that the teacher sees more than the student.42:T538,The paper revisits the derivation of DPO from KL-regularized RLHF under the Bradley-Terry preference model. The critical step is the substitution of the RLHF-optimal policy into the reward parameterization. The authors argue that this substitution is valid only if the optimal policy itself respects the observed preference ordering. RLHF does not guarantee this, because its objective trades off reward against KL divergence from the reference model. When the reference model strongly prefers the wrong response, the KL term can pull the optimum toward the wrong ordering. Under this violation, DPO no longer optimizes the same objective as RLHF: it optimizes relative advantage over the reference policy. The paper formalizes a pathological solution region where DPO loss can decrease while the learned policy still prefers dispreferred responses. To address this, it introduces Constrained Preference Optimization, which augments the RLHF objective with explicit preference constraints. The paper also gives a geometric view through soft margin ranking loss: DPO can induce an effective margin that becomes negative, while CPO enforces non-negative effective margins. A conservative variant, E-CPOC, is proposed to avoid explicit reward modeling while maintaining a formal connection to constrained RLHF via a loss-to-delta argument.43:T53f,The method has a diagnostic part and an intervention part. For diagnosis, the authors define Advantage Collapse Rate, the fraction of training batches whose group-relative advantages are fully or nearly degenerate. In GRPO, advantages are computed by standardizing rewards within a group. If all rewards are identical, the group mean equals each reward and the group standard deviation is zero, so every advantage becomes zero. Near-homogeneous rewards lead to the same problem in practice. ACR is cheap because it reuses statistics already computed during GRPO training.

For mitigation, they introduce Adaptive Virtual Sample Policy Optimization (AVSPO). The key idea is not to redesign the policy objective or add a critic, but to inject virtual reward samples when collapse is detected, so that a non-zero relative comparison signal can still be formed. This creates usable advantages for groups that would otherwise be gradient-dead. The paper emphasizes that AVSPO is plug-and-play: no extra response generation and no additional LLM forward passes are required. Compared with critic-based PPO or GAE, it avoids a value network; compared with process reward models, it does not require step-level supervision; compared with entropy regularization, it directly targets reward homogeneity rather than applying generic exploration pressure.44:T43d,For a quick read, focus on three parts. First, read the problem setup carefully to distinguish this from mode collapse or reward hacking; the paper is about zeroed group-relative advantages under homogeneous rewards. Second, inspect the ACR definition and the correlation plots linking early ACR to final performance; that is the most informative part of the paper. Third, read the AVSPO algorithm section with one question in mind: how virtual samples alter advantage computation without requiring extra model passes.

If time is limited to 20–30 minutes, a good path is: Introduction → the GRPO preliminaries and collapse derivation → the main ACR correlation figure → the main table comparing collapse rate and accuracy. If considering implementation, then read the ablations on model scale, benchmark variation, and out-of-domain behavior. The main point to scrutinize is whether virtual reward injection introduces bias or over-amplifies weak distinctions in high-accuracy regimes; the paper provides empirical reassurance, but that remains the key place to probe further.45:T5bb,The core contribution is an evaluation framework, not a new reasoning objective. The authors define structural validity criteria for reasoning traces: whether a generated response contains a complete, non-empty, parseable reasoning segment. Invalid traces are further decomposed into empty, missing, and truncated cases. They then combine these structural outcomes with task performance through reasoning-conditioned pass@1, which measures task success only on examples where a valid reasoning trace is present. This disentangles two factors: whether the model still has useful reasoning ability when it reasons explicitly, and whether it still reliably emits explicit reasoning at all.

To make this usable across models, they build ThinkPack, a lightweight toolkit for model-agnostic prompt construction, trace parsing, validation, metric computation, and loss masking. This matters because open reasoning models differ substantially in templates and trace formats, and evaluation can otherwise be confounded by model-specific parsing assumptions.

For mitigation, the paper compares standard SFT, loss masking, and teacher distillation. The masking strategy is intentionally lightweight: instead of generating new traces, it removes loss from regions where reasoning is absent, so the model is not directly rewarded for omitting the trace. Distillation is more direct but requires additional trace generation and is less convenient for private or specialized data.46:T494,The experiments cover four open-weight reasoning models with different training pipelines and reasoning formats. The models are fine-tuned on standard instruction–response data without explicit traces, and evaluated on science QA, mathematical reasoning, and code generation. The main empirical pattern is clear: ordinary SFT can rapidly reduce the rate of valid reasoning traces, while final-answer performance may remain partially intact for some time. This means answer accuracy alone can hide substantial degradation in explicit reasoning behavior.

An especially useful observation is that in several settings, pass@1 conditioned on valid reasoning remains relatively high even as the valid-trace rate drops sharply. That suggests the model has not fully lost reasoning ability; instead, it is increasingly failing to externalize it. On mitigation, simple masking often preserves valid reasoning behavior much better than standard fine-tuning while maintaining strong task performance. Teacher distillation also helps, but at higher cost. The most important result is therefore the systematic divergence between structural reasoning metrics and answer-only metrics.47:T46a,This is best read as an evaluation paper. Start with the problem definition and make sure the target is clear: the paper measures the presence and structural integrity of explicit traces, not their faithfulness or semantic quality. Then read the metric section carefully, especially the definitions of valid, empty, missing, and truncated traces, and the construction of reasoning-conditioned pass@1. After that, inspect the main training curves or tables showing how valid-trace rate and answer accuracy diverge under standard SFT. Finally, read the mitigation comparison between masking and distillation.

If you only have about 25 minutes, read in this order: problem setup → metric design → main empirical results → mitigation section. If you are considering adoption, spend extra time on ThinkPack and the parser assumptions, since robustness across templates is central to whether the framework transfers cleanly. One important boundary to keep in mind: the paper shows that explicit trace behavior can disappear, but it does not by itself establish how much internal latent reasoning ability is lost at the same time.48:T7a2,PRISM introduces model preference into the construction of the target direction. The target behavior is defined through paired anchors: for each prompt q, there is a target-positive response y_q^p and a target-negative response y_q^n. A standard influence-based method would typically average the gradient differences g(q, y_q^p) - g(q, y_q^n) across target pairs and then score each candidate SFT example z by how well its gradient aligns with that averaged direction.

The paper argues that this uniform aggregation is too crude. If the current model already assigns relatively high probability to some positive anchors, those target pairs correspond to nearby behavioral directions that can be strengthened efficiently with a small amount of data. Distant target pairs may still describe the desired behavior semantically, but they are weaker guides for local data selection under a fixed budget.

Concretely, PRISM computes a preference score π_q for each target pair, defined from the model’s normalized probability of the positive anchor relative to the negative anchor. It then uses π_q to weight the gradient difference for that pair, producing a preference-aware target direction. Candidate training examples are scored by the similarity between their influence signal and this weighted target direction. High-scoring examples are those expected to move the model in a direction that increases preference for the desired behavior.

The paper also provides a first-order local analysis: near the current parameter point, PRISM approximates the direction that most efficiently increases target preference per unit data budget. Uniform averaging, by mixing high-preference and low-preference targets, can point away from the behavior that the current model can most readily amplify. A practical advantage is that the same ranking machinery supports both retention of useful data for efficient fine-tuning and removal of harmful data for repair.49:T589,The experiments cover two settings. The first is capability-oriented data selection, where the target behavior corresponds to knowledge ability, mathematical reasoning, and safety guardrails. The second is safety-critical SFT repair, where the goal is to remove a limited amount of harmful training data to reduce undesirable behavior. The main empirical claim is consistent across both settings: under the same keep budget or removal budget, PRISM selects data that yields better target-behavior improvement or more effective safety repair.

The paper emphasizes two patterns. First, the gains hold across multiple model families and scales, suggesting that the effect is not tied to one specific backbone. Second, the ablations indicate that the main benefit comes from preference-aware weighting of target examples rather than from replacing the influence-scoring machinery itself. In other words, the key idea is not a more elaborate influence estimator, but a better target representation that depends on the current model state.

The provided full-text excerpt does not include the complete result tables, so it is not possible to quote reliable parameter counts, dataset sizes, or exact improvement margins. What is clear is that the evaluation is systematic: multiple tasks, multiple models, and both retain/remove use cases are tested, with ablations aligned to the central hypothesis about target weighting.4a:T73c,The method is simple, but the supervision-path argument is the core insight. The paper first formalizes standard agent SFT over a k-turn trajectory: for the first k-1 turns, the loss is applied only to reasoning and action tokens, while tool responses o_t are masked. This means o_t mainly receives gradients through nearby action prediction, whereas answer-relevant gradients must travel through many intermediate turns and become weak. In effect, the model is optimized for local tool-use decisions, not for preserving answer-critical features from earlier observations.

ACC rewrites the trajectory into a long-context QA training pair. It compiles the original question together with tool responses and environment observations gathered across turns, then uses the verified final answer as the target. This turns previously masked observations into explicit conditioning evidence for answer prediction. The model is therefore trained on a direct mapping from question plus distributed evidence to answer, instead of relying on indirect supervision through intermediate actions.

A few implementation choices matter. The paper uses answer-verified trajectories, which reduces noise from failed or misleading agent runs. It evaluates the idea on three agent families—search, SWE, and SQL—to show the recipe is not tied to one environment. ACC is also positioned as complementary to existing long-context extension methods rather than a replacement for them.

The mechanism analysis is one of the more interesting parts. After ACC training, the model exhibits task-adaptive attention restructuring and clearer expert specialization in the MoE backbone. That suggests the gain is not merely memorizing a new data format; the internal routing and retrieval behavior changes in a task-dependent way when the model needs to integrate distant evidence.4b:T4fb,The main results are on two long-range dependency benchmarks, MRCR and GraphWalks. ACC-trained Qwen3-30B-A3B reaches 68.3 on MRCR, a gain of +18.1, and 77.5 on GraphWalks, a gain of +7.6. The paper highlights that these results are comparable to Qwen3-235B-A22B on those tasks, which is the strongest practical message: targeted supervision can let a much smaller model approach a much larger one on specific long-context integration abilities.
